Functors induced by comma categories
Suddhasattwa Das

Abstract
Category theory provides a collective description of many arrangements in mathematics, such as topological spaces, Banach spaces and game theory. Within this collective description, the perspective from any individual member of the collection is provided by its associated left or right slice. The assignment of slices to objects extends to a functor from the base category, into the category of categories. Slice categories are a special case of the more general notion of comma categories. Comma categories are created when two categories 𝒜 \mathcal{A} and ℬ \mathcal{B} transform into a common third category 𝒞 \mathcal{C} , via functors F , G F,G . Such arrangements denoted as [ F ; G ] \left[F\;;\;G\right] abound in mathematics, and provide a categorical interpretation of many constructions in dynamics. Objects in this category are morphisms between objects of 𝒜 \mathcal{A} and ℬ \mathcal{B} , via the functors F , G F,G . We show that these objects also have a natural interpretation as functors between slice categories of 𝒜 \mathcal{A} and ℬ \mathcal{B} . Thus even though 𝒜 \mathcal{A} and ℬ \mathcal{B} may have completely disparate structures, morphisms in 𝒞 \mathcal{C} lead to functors between their respective slices. We present this relation in the form of a functor from 𝒞 \mathcal{C} into the category of left slices. The proof of our main result requires a deeper look into associated categories, in which the objects themselves are various commuting diagrams.



1  Introduction

Our discussion is based on the following general arrangement of categories and functors :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

(1)

The comma category
\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]
built from this arrangement has as objects

ob\left(\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]\right):=\left\{\left(a,b,\phi\right)\;:\;a\in ob(\mathcal{A}),\,b\in ob(\mathcal{B}),\,\phi\in\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}\left(\alpha a;\beta b\right)\right\},

and the morphisms comprise of pairs

\left\{(f,g)\;:\;f\in\Hom(D),\,g\in\Hom(E)\right\}
such that the following commutation holds :

Thus
\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]
may be interpreted as a the category of bindings between
\alpha,\beta
, via their common codomain
\mathcal{C}
. Comma categories contain as sub-structures, the original categories
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
, via the
forgetful
functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

whose action on morphisms in
\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]
can be described as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\alpha a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\alpha a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\beta b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\beta b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha f}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Comma categories prevail all over category theory and mathematics in general, such as graph theory
(
goguen1991mnfst
, e.g.)
, in the theory of lenses and fibrations
(
johnson2012lenses
, e.g.)
, iterative algebras
(
adamek2006iterative
, e.g.)
, stochastic processes
(
cho2019disintegration
, e.g.)
, and mathematical logic
(
streicher2004intro
;
pech2018fraisse
, e.g.)
. If a category can be presented as a comma category, then one obtains additional results to prove the existence of limits and colimits
(
GoguenBurstall1984a
;
Das2023CatEntropy
, e.g.)
. Two examples are as follows :

Example 1
.

DasSuda2024recon
Suppose
\mathcal{T}
is a semigroup, then it is representable as a 1-object category. Let
\Topo
be the category of topological spaces and continuous maps. Then the class of topological dynamics is the functor category
\mathbb{F}\left(\mathcal{T}\;;\;\Topo\right)
. Now let
\left[\text{Euc}\right]
be the subgroup of
\Topo
comprised only of Euclidean spaces. Then the following arrangement is of the nature of (
1
) :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}\left(\mathcal{T}\;;\;\Topo\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\text{Euc}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Topo}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dom}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}

leads to a comma category. The domain functor
\dom
above assigns to every dynamical system its domain. A typical object in this category is a dynamical systems
(\Omega,\Phi^{t})
along with a measurement
\phi:\Omega\to\real^{d}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
d
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

Example 2
.

Let
\left[\text{Aff}\right]
be the category of vector spaces and affine maps, and
\left[\text{FinVect}\right]
be the category of finite dimensional vector spaces and linear maps. Then the following arrangement

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\text{FinVect}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\text{FinVect}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\text{Aff}\right]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}

leads to a comma category in which the objects are affine maps between finite dimensional vector spaces, and the morphisms are pairs of linear transformations that perform a change of variables. This category is the key to a generalized notion of nullity
HSYprevalence1992
.

A particular instance of comma categories are
slice categories
. Henceforth, we shall use the symbol
\star
to denote the category with a single object with no non-trivial morphism. Take any category
\mathcal{X}
, and an object
x
in it. This object may be interpreted by a unique functor from
\star
to
\mathcal{X}
, which we shall also denote by
\star\xrightarrow{x}\mathcal{X}
. Now set

\mathcal{B}=\star,\,\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{X},\,\beta=x,\,\alpha=\Id_{\mathcal{X}},

in (
1
). The resulting comma category
\left[\Id_{\mathcal{X}}\;;\;x\right]
is known as the left slice of
x
in
\mathcal{X}
, and will be denoted more briefly as
\left[\mathcal{X}\;;\;x\right]
. A typical morphism in this category is shown below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

The objects are the morphisms shown in blue, and a morphism
\phi
from
f
to
f^{\prime}
is a morphism
\phi:y\to y^{\prime}
such that the above commutation holds. One can similarly define the right slice of an object within its category. An important example of a slice category is the right slice of the pointed space in the category
\Topo
of topological spaces. This corresponds to the category of pointed topological spaces. If
\mathcal{X}
is a preorder category, the left or right slice of an object
x
is the
down-set
or
up-set
of the object. If
\mathcal{X}
is the collection of subsets of a superset
\mathcal{U}
ordered by inclusion, then the left slice of any subset
x
of
\mathcal{U}
is the power set of
x
, also ordered by inclusion.

Example 3
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be any topological space. Then its left slice
\left[\Topo\;;\;\mathcal{U}\right]
in
\Topo
is the category formed by the collection of all continuous maps into
\mathcal{U}
.

Example 4
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a set and
2^{\mathcal{U}}
be the power set of
\mathcal{U}
. Thus
2^{\mathcal{U}}
is a preorder and a category. Then the left slice of any set
S\subset\mathcal{U}
in
2^{\mathcal{U}}
is the power set of
S
.

Example 5
.

More generally, let
\mathcal{C}
be any category and
\mathcal{C}_{mono}
be the subcategory comprising only of monomorphisms. Then the left slice of any object
c
of
\mathcal{C}
in
\mathcal{C}_{mono}
is the
subobject category
of
c
.

The forgetful functors inbuilt into comma categories may also be arranged into diagrams similar to (
1
). Consider the following more abstract example :

.

) one has the following diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

where
b
is an object of
\mathcal{B}
and
\star
is the 1-point category. This arrangement creates the comma category
\left[\pi_{2}^{\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}\;;\;b\right]
, whose objects are

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-13.09856pt}{-20.09082pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right\}

(2)

Every such object creates an L-shaped diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha a^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta b^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta b^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta g}

This is a diagram within
\mathcal{C}
in which one arrow
f
is drawn from
\mathcal{C}
, while another
g
is drawn from
\mathcal{B}
.

Example
6
reveals how various diagrams following a certain pattern create a category of its own. Example
6
is a generalization of the more concrete Example
1
. The comma category (
2
) will play a significant role in the next section, where we state some technical results. The proof of Theorem
1
require a study of how the objects of comma categories create a functor between the slice categories sitting at the end-points of the arrows.

Yet another important manifestation of comma categories are
arrow
categories. If we set

\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{X},\,\alpha=\beta=\Id_{\mathcal{X}},

in (
1
), then the resulting comma category
\left[\Id_{\mathcal{X}}\;;\;\Id_{\mathcal{X}}\right]
is called the arrow category of
\mathcal{X}
, and is denoted by
\text{Arrow}\left[\mathcal{X}\right]
. The objects in this category are the arrows or morphisms in
\mathcal{X}
. A morphism between two arrows
x\xrightarrow{f}x^{\prime}
and
y\xrightarrow{g}y^{\prime}
is a pair of morphisms
x\xrightarrow{\phi}y
and
x^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\phi^{\prime}}y^{\prime}
such that the following commutation holds :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Thus
\text{Arrow}\left[\mathcal{X}\right]
reveals how the arrows of
\mathcal{X}
are bound to each other via the commutation relations in
\mathcal{X}
. Some important examples are

Example 7
.

The arrow category
\text{Arrow}\left[\Topo\right]
in
\Topo
corresponds to the category of topological pairs.

Example 8
.

The arrow category
\text{Arrow}\left[\left[\text{Vec}\right]\right]
in
\left[\text{Vec}\right]
corresponds to the category of linear maps, with linear change of variables serving as morphisms.

Example 9
.

Recall the notations in Examples
3
and
4
. Let
\mathcal{U}
be a topological space. Take
\mathcal{X}
to be the power set
2^{\mathcal{U}}
and
\mathcal{Y}
to be the subcategory of
\Topo
formed by all topological subspaces of
\mathcal{U}
. Then one has an obvious inclusion
\iota:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
which satisfy all of Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
. Note that the comma category
\left[\iota\;;\;\iota\right]
has the same objects as the arrow category
\text{Arrow}\left[\mathcal{Y}\right]
, but a different collection of morphisms. Any object
F\in\text{Arrow}\left[\mathcal{Y}\right]
is a continuous map between topological spaces
F:X\to Y
.

Comma, slice and arrow categories thus represent finer structures present within categories, and also how objects from different categories assemble together to produce more complex categories. The language of comma categories contributes to the universality of category theory as an alternate formulation for descriptive set theory
Lawvere1963semantics
. In the next section, we present the main result which analyzes how the objects in a comma category could induce functors between left slices.

2  Main results

We now shift our attention to a particular instance of the arrangement of (
1
) of the form
\mathcal{X}\xrightarrow{\iota}\mathcal{Y}\xleftarrow{\iota}\mathcal{X}
. More precisely

Assumption 1
.

There are complete categories
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, with initial objects
0_{\mathcal{X}}
and
0_{\mathcal{Y}}
respectively, and there is a continuous functor
\iota:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
such that
0_{\mathcal{Y}}=\iota\left(0_{\mathcal{X}}\right)
, and
\iota
is injective on objects.

Let
\left\llbracket\text{Cat}\right\rrbracket
denote the category of small categories. Let
\text{LeftSlice}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)
denote the full subcategory of
\left\llbracket\text{Cat}\right\rrbracket
generated by the slice categories

\left[\mathcal{X}\;;\;\Omega\right],\quad\Omega\in ob(\mathcal{X}).

Let
\iota(\mathcal{X})
denote the full subcategory of
\mathcal{Y}
generated by objects of
\iota
. Note that the arrows in
\iota(\mathcal{X})
are precisely the objects of
\left[\iota\;;\;\iota\right]
. Recall that a morphism
f
in any category is said to be
surjective
or equivalently, an
epimorphism
, if for any composable morphisms
g,g^{\prime}
, if
g^{\prime}f=gf
, then
g=g^{\prime}
. Similarly, a morphism is said to be
injective
or equivalently, a
monomorphism
, if for any composable morphisms
g,g^{\prime}
, if
fg^{\prime}=fg
, then
g=g^{\prime}
. Our next important realization will be that the induced morphisms
\tau_{F}(f)
are surjective. We need the following assumption :

Assumption 2
.

For every monomorphism
f
in
\mathcal{Y}
, there are morphisms
g
in
\mathcal{Y}
and
h
in
\mathcal{Y}
such that
f=(\iota h)g
.

Assumption 3
.

The image under
\iota
of every morphism in
\mathcal{X}
is injective in
\mathcal{Y}
.

Assumption 4
.

Any morphism in
\mathcal{Y}
which is both surjective and injective is an isomorphism.

Assumption
4
is satisfied in categories such as topoi
Goldblatt2014topoi
;
Freyd1972topoi
;
Brook1975fin
.
\left\llbracket\text{Set}\right\rrbracket
,
\Topo
are two prime examples of topoi. Another important category in which Assumption
4
is satisfied is
\left[\text{Group}\right]
, the category of groups and homomorphisms.

Theorem 1
.

Let Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
hold. Then there is a functor

\iota(\mathcal{X})\xrightarrow{\Dyn}\text{LeftSlice}\left(\mathcal{X}\right),

which maps every object
\Omega\in\mathcal{X}
to the left slice
\left[\mathcal{X}\;;\;\Omega\right]
. It maps an arrow
\iota\Omega\xrightarrow{F}\iota\Omega^{\prime}
into a functor

\left[\mathcal{X}\;;\;\Omega\right]\xrightarrow{\tau_{F}}\left[\mathcal{X}\;;\;\Omega^{\prime}\right]

such that for every
A\xrightarrow{a}\Omega\in\left[\mathcal{X}\;;\;\Omega\right]
,
\tau_{F}(a)
creates a commutation square

for some

. Moreover, this square is universal in the sense that for any slice-object

and any morphism
f:\iota A\to\iota B^{\prime}
, if the blue commutation square shown below holds

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

(4)

then there is a unique morphism
\phi
which factors the outer commutation loop into the inner commutation loop.

One can interpret a category as a context, such as topology, Markov processes or topoi. The left slice objects in
\text{LeftSlice}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)
reside within the structure of
\mathcal{X}
. Theorem
1
demonstrates a manner in which morphisms from a different category
\mathcal{Y}
naturally creates functors between the various left-slices of
\mathcal{X}
.

The statement of Theorem
1
is pictorial. It says that beginning with any L-shaped diagram as shown on the left of (
3
), one has the commuting diagram as shown on the right of (
3
). Moreover, this diagram is universal in the sense that all other possible commutation squares over the L-diagram can be recovered from it, as shown in (
4
).

There are numerous examples of the arrangement of Theorem
1
all over the different branches of mathematics. One of the most important applications of Theorem
1
is the following :

Example 10
.

Recall the notations in Example
9
. Any object
F\in\left[\iota\;;\;\iota\right]
is a continuous map between topological spaces
F:X\to Y
. Then
\tau_{F}
can be interpreted to be the induced map from the power set
\left[\iota\;;\;X\right]
of
X
, into the power set
\left[\iota\;;\;Y\right]
of
Y
. The correspondence between
F
and
\tau_{F}
is itself functorial. Overall, we have a functor
\Dyn:\left[\iota\;;\;\iota\right]\to\text{LeftSlice}\left(2^{\mathcal{U}}\right)
. Note that
\text{LeftSlice}\left(2^{\mathcal{U}}\right)
is the category in which each object is a power set of some
S\subset\mathcal{U}
, and morphisms are inclusion preserving maps between these power sets.

Example
10
can be generalized based on the following observation :

Lemma 2.1
.

Given any complete category
\mathcal{C}
, the subcategory
\mathcal{C}_{mono}
formed by monomorphisms is complete, and has the same initial object as
\mathcal{C}
.

Lemma
2.1
and Theorem
1
have the following important consequence :

Corollary 2
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is a complete category in which every morphism which is both a monormorphism and epimorphism is an isomorphism. Then :

1.

The inclusion
\iota:\mathcal{C}_{mono}\to\mathcal{C}
satisfies Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
.

2.

There is a functor
\Dyn
from
\mathcal{C}
into the full subcategory of
\left\llbracket\text{Cat}\right\rrbracket
spanned by the sub-object categories of
\mathcal{C}
.

Some examples of categories
\mathcal{C}
which satisfy the conditions of Corollary
2
are
\left\llbracket\text{Set}\right\rrbracket
,
\Topo
and
\left[\text{Group}\right]
. As a result we have the following instances of Corollary
2
:

Example 11
.

Continuing the discussion in Example
8
, any linear map
A:U\to V
between vector spaces induces a mapping between the subspaces of
U
and
V
respectively. This correspondence is a functor from
\left[\text{Vec}\right]
to the category of collections of vector subspaces.

As a slight variation to Example
11
we have :

Example 12
.

Take
\mathcal{X}=\left[\text{Vec}\right]_{mono}
and
\mathcal{Y}=\left[\text{Aff}\right]
. The initial objects
0_{\mathcal{X}}
into
0_{\mathcal{Y}}
are both the zero-dimensional vector space. Note that there is an inclusion functor
\iota:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
which maps
0_{\mathcal{X}}
into
0_{\mathcal{Y}}
. Thus according to Theorem
1
any affine map is functorially related to a map between the collection of vector subspaces of the corresponding spaces.

If
\mathcal{X}
is a preorder, a left slice
\left[\mathcal{X}\;;\;a\right]
can be interpreted as the set
\left\{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}\;:\;a^{\prime}\leq a\right\}
. This is a sub-preorder of
\mathcal{X}
, and is called the
down-set
of
a
.

Corollary 3
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a complete preorder, and
\iota:\mathcal{X}\to\left[\text{Group}\right]
a functor that maps
1_{\mathcal{X}}
into the trivial group with one object. Let
a,b
be objects in
\mathcal{X}
and
F:\iota(a)\to\iota(b)
be a group homomorphism. Then
F
induces an order preserving map
\tau_{F}
between the down-sets
\left[\mathcal{X}\;;\;a\right]
and
\left[\mathcal{X}\;;\;b\right]
. Moreover this correspondence is functorial.

An example of
\mathcal{X},\iota
from Corollary
3
is when
\mathcal{X}
is a cellular complex, comprised of a collection of inclusions between face maps. Recall that singular homology is a functor

\text{Homology}:\Topo\to\left[\text{Group}\right],

mapping each topological space into its sequence of homology groups.

Example 13
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a simplicial complex of dimension
n
, comprised of simplices of various dimensions from
0
to
n
. Each simplex of dimension less than
n
is included as a face map of a simplex of a higher dimension. Thus
\mathcal{X}
is a preorder with a finite number of objects and morphisms contained within
\Topo
. Then for any two faces
a,b
of
\mathcal{X}
, the down-sets
\left[\mathcal{X}\;;\;a\right]
and
\left[\mathcal{X}\;;\;b\right]
are the sub-simplexes of these faces. Any group homomorphism

F:\text{Homology}(a)\to\text{Homology}(b)

induces a unique simplicial map between these sub-complexes whose induced map between the homology groups is precisely
F
.

The last statement in the example above is supported by the commutation in (
4
).

Example
1
presented a functorial interpretation of dynamical systems. Category theoretic reformulations of dynamical systems have become of increasing interest due to the simplicity of presentation of many of the deeper results in dynamical systems theory
(
MossPerrone2022ergdc
;
Suda2022Poincare
;
Das2023CatEntropy
;
DasSuda2024recon
, e.g.)
. This is the advantage provided by the constructive / synthetic language of category theory, as opposed to the descriptive nature of set-theoretic language. The new challenge that emerges is that many basic definitions which are trivial in a set-theoretic presentation, becomes harder to present in a category theoretic setting. A prime example is the notion of an orbit. One can associate orbits to both topological, smooth, or measurable dynamical systems. However, the notion of orbit itself is as a minimal object in
\left\llbracket\text{Set}\right\rrbracket
satisfying certain properties. In a category theoretic presentation, objects lose all their inner details and are presented as part of a larger collection. The focus shifts from the content of objects, to their relational and compositional structure. Thus orbits cannot be simply defined to be a union of successive images. A categorical definition of orbits is an major gap in the category theoretic reformulation of dynamics, and the functor
\Dyn
discovered in Theorem
1
fills this gap.

Example 14
.

Recall the notations from Examples
3
,
4
and
10
. Also recall from Example
1
the interpretation of a topological dynamical system as a functor
\Phi:\mathcal{T}\to\Topo
, where
\mathcal{T}
is a semigroup such as
\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\mathbb{Z}
or . Let us assume without loss of generality that the domain of the dynamics is a subspace of
\mathcal{U}
. Let
\text{Topo}(\mathcal{U})
denote the subcategory of
\Topo
generated by all topological subspaces of
\mathcal{U}
. Then one has the following composable sequence of functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Topo}(\mathcal{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{LeftSlice}\left(2^{\mathcal{U}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dyn}

The composition of these functors is a set-theoretic dynamical system with time semigroup
\mathcal{T}
. Thus the interpretation of a topological dynamical system as a set-theoretic dynamical system is also functorial.

Recall that given a dynamical system
\Phi^{t}:\Omega\to\Omega
on a space
\Omega
, the orbit of a subset
S\subseteq\Omega
is the union
\cup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Phi^{t}(S)
. Alternatively it can be defined to be the smallest subset of
\Omega
that contains all the images
\Phi^{t}(S)
. This definition is very suitable for a category theoretic presentation. Note that the composite functor
\tilde{\Phi}:\Dyn\circ\Phi
from Example
14
leads to a functor
2^{\Omega}\times\mathcal{T}\to 2^{\Omega}
. Then the orbit of
\Phi
is the functor shown in the diagram below :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2^{\Omega}\times\mathcal{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2^{\Omega}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2^{\Omega}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2^{\Omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\proj_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Phi}}
Orbit

(5)

The bottom horizontal arrow is created by a construction called a right-
Kan extension
. Kan extensions are a purely diagrammatic / categorical notion, and is elaborated in Section
7
. Example
14
and Diagram (
5
) is a succinct but precise way of stating the following facts :

Corollary 4
.

Every topological dynamical system on a subspace of the universe
\mathcal{U}
is a functor
\Phi:\mathcal{T}\to\Topo(\mathcal{U})
. It leads to the following notions :

1.

This functor combines with
\Dyn
from Theorem
1
to get a dynamical system
\mathcal{T}\to\text{LeftSlice}\left(2^{\mathcal{U}}\right)
.

2.

This leads to a functor
\tilde{\Phi}:2^{\Omega}\times\mathcal{T}\to 2^{\Omega}
.

3.

The existence of the orbit functor follows from the existence of a right Kan extension, as shown in (
5
).

4.

The minimality of the orbit follows from the universal property of a right Kan extension.

These examples and Corollaries
2
–
4
highlight the prevalence of the arrangement described in Theorem
1
. Theorem
1
is proved in Section
4
, as a consequence of Theorems
5
,
6
and
8
. See Figure
1
for an outline of the proof of Theorem
1
, and how the other main results fit into the proof. Over the course of the next two chapters, we examine several finer aspects of Theorem
1
.

3  The induced functor between slices

Theorem
1
was about the comma category
\left[\iota\;;\;\iota\right]
which is a special case of (
1
). We now make an assumption on (
1
), which turns out to be a generalization of Assumption
1
.

Assumption 5
.

The category
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
from (
1
) are complete, the functor
\beta
is continuous. Categories
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{C}
have initial elements
0_{\mathcal{A}}
and
0_{\mathcal{C}}
respectively, and
\alpha\left(0_{\mathcal{A}}\right)=0_{\mathcal{C}}

Our first result arises from the simple situation when two objects
a,b
are picked from
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
in (
1
), mapped into
\mathcal{C}
, and bound by a morphism
\phi
in
\mathcal{C}
. The objects
a,b
have their own left-slice categories in
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
, which are independent of each other as well as
\mathcal{C}
. We shall see how the morphism
\phi
induces a functor between these two categories.

Theorem 5

(Induced functor)
.

Assume the arrangement of (
1
), and let Assumption
5
hold. Fix an object
\alpha a\xrightarrow{F}\beta b
of the comma category
\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]
. Then there is a functor
\tau_{F}:\left[\mathcal{A}\;;\;a\right]\to\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]
such that for any object
a^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}a
in
\left[\mathcal{A}\;;\;a\right]
, there is an object
b^{\prime}\xrightarrow{g}b
in
\left[\mathcal{B}\;;\;b\right]
, such that the following commutation holds

then there is a unique morphism
b^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\phi}b^{\prime\prime}
such that the commutation on the right holds.

Thus the correspondence
\tau_{F}
associates to every object
f
in the left slice
\left[\mathcal{A}\;;\;a\right]
an object
\tau_{F}(f)
in the comma category
\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]
. This object
\tau_{F}(f)
itself is an arrow in
\mathcal{C}
and creates a commutation square involving
f
and
F
.

Remark

The minimality so described is hardly surprising, since whenever a commutation such as (
6
) holds, the following commutation also holds

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta b^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha a^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta g\circ\tau_{F}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{F}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta\Id_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

This diagram is a special case of the second claim of Theorem
5
, with
\tilde{F}=\beta g\circ\tau_{F}(f)
and
\phi=g^{\prime}
.

Remark

One of the consequences of Theorem
5
and the commutation in (
6
) is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathcal{A}\;;\;a\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}

This means that the domain of the arrow
\tau_{F}(f)
is the same as the domain of the arrow
f
.

Remark

Any object
a^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}a
in
\left[\mathcal{A}\;;\;a\right]
is sent by
\pi_{1}
into
a^{\prime}
, whereas it is sent by
\tau_{F}
into
\tau_{F}(f)
, which is then sent by
\pi_{1}
into
a^{\prime}
. This commutation be extended as follows :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathcal{A}\;;\;a\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathcal{B}\;;\;b\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dyn_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

(7)

The diagram presents a new functor
\Dyn_{F}
between the slice categories associated to the terminal points of the comma object
F
.

Recall the category
\left[\pi_{2}^{\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}\;;\;b\right]
(
2
) presented in Example
6
. The compound objects in
\left[\pi_{2}^{\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}\;;\;b\right]
lead to a projection functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\pi_{2}^{\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}\;;\;b\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathcal{B}\;;\;b\right]}
Restrict

Both Theorem
5
and (
7
) are consequences of the following more general result :

Theorem 6
.

Under the same assumptions as Theorem
5
and the category in (
2
). Then there is a functor

\bar{\tau}_{F}:\left[\mathcal{A}\;;\;a\right]\to\left[\pi_{2}^{\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}\;;\;b\right]

such that the functors
\tau_{F}
and
\Dyn_{F}
are created via composition :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathcal{A}\;;\;a\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathcal{B}\;;\;b\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{UR}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\pi_{2}^{\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}\;;\;b\right]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dyn_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\tau}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
Restrict

(8)

Theorem
6
jointly implies the statements of Theorem
5
and (
7
). Theorem
6
is proved in Section
8
. See Figure
1
for a summary of the various results and their logical connections.

Remark

When
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{C}
in (
1
), and
\alpha=\beta=\Id_{\mathcal{A}}
, then
\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]
is just the arrow category
\text{Arrow}\left[\mathcal{A}\right]
. Any object
a\xrightarrow{F}b
in this category induces a functor between the slice categories :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.99,0.27,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.99,0.27,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

The yellow and blue arrows represent different objects in the respective slice categories, and the red arrows represent morphisms between these objects. The diagram on the right is obtained from the left by simply composing with
F
. This functorial relation coincides with
\tau_{F}
.

Remark

While
\tau_{F}
has a simple interpretation when all the functors in (
1
) are identities, determining an induced functor in the more general setting is not trivial. One notable approach relies on the existence of special factorization systems
(
adamek1990abstract
;
FreydKelly1972cont1
, e.g.)
. This approach has been extended to an axiomatic study of topology
Schlomiuk1970topo
;
ClementinoGiuliTholen1996
;
DikranjanGiuli1987closure
;
DikranjanTholen2013clos
.

In the next section, we look more closely at the correspondence between
F
and
\tau_{F}
.

4  Algebra of induced functors

Theorem
5
presents how an object in a comma category induces a functor between the left- slices associated to the two endpoints of the object. The functor is realized through morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
binding an object in a left slice object in
\mathcal{A}
, to a left slice object in
\mathcal{B}
. In this section we shift our attention to the case when
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{B}
. In that case all the left slices involved are within the same category.

Theorem 7
.

Assumptions
1
and
2
hold. Then the induced morphisms
\tau_{F}(f)
from Theorem
5
are surjective.

The proof requires the following lemma :

Lemma 4.1
.

In any category, an equalizer is injective.

Proof of Theorem
7

To prove surjectivity we need to show that for any pair of morphisms
\alpha,\beta:\iota B\to C
, if
\alpha\circ\tau_{F}{f}=\beta\circ\tau_{F}{f}
then
\alpha=\beta
. Since
\mathcal{Y}
is complete it has equalizers. Consider the following diagram in which the equalizer of
\alpha,\beta
has been shown.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Eq}(\alpha,\beta)"}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{F}(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Since the equalizer is by definition, the universal morphism
\gamma
such that
\beta\gamma=\alpha\gamma
, the morphism
\tau_{F}(f)
must factor through the equalizer via the morphism
\phi
as shown. Now by Lemma
4.1
, the morphism
\text{Eq}(\alpha,\beta)
is injective. By Assumption
2
,
\text{Eq}(\alpha,\beta)
factorizes as shown below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Eq}(\alpha,\beta)"}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{F}(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

This commutation diagram can be joined with the definition of
\tau_{F}(f)
to get

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota\Omega^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Eq}(\alpha,\beta)"}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota(b\circ\psi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{F}(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

By the universality of
\tau_{F}(f)
, the morphism
\psi
must be an isomorphism. This would mean that
\text{Eq}(\alpha,\beta)
is an isomorphism too. This in turn implies that
\alpha=\beta
, which was our goal. This completes the proof of Theorem
7
. ∎

We have been examining the particular instance of (
1
) when
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{X}
, and
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{Y}
, and both functors
\alpha,\beta
are
\iota:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
. In that case, the diagram (
7
) becomes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\iota\;;\;\iota\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathcal{X}\;;\;\Omega\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathcal{X}\;;\;\Omega^{\prime}\right]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dyn_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

One of the consequences of equating
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
is that the functor described by Theorems
5
and
6
are between slices of the same category. Our goal is to investigate the composability of the horizontal arrows in the bottom row. To gain a precise footing, we assume

: Functor from codomain category into category of left slices

: induced functor between left slice categories

)

Theorem 8

(Compositionality of induced functors)
.

Suppose Assumptions
1

3
and
4
hold. Then there is a functor

\iota(\mathcal{X})\xrightarrow{\tau}\text{LeftSlice}\left(\mathcal{X}\right),

which maps an arrow
\iota\Omega\xrightarrow{F}\iota\Omega^{\prime}
into
\left[\mathcal{X}\;;\;\Omega\right]\xrightarrow{\tau_{F}}\left[\mathcal{X}\;;\;\Omega^{\prime}\right]
.

The upper commuting loop is the statement of Theorem
8
. The outer commutating loop, along with the two smaller loops are a consequence of (
7
).

Lemma 4.2
.

In any category
\mathcal{C}
, if
f,g
are two composable morphisms such that
f,g\circ f
are surjective, then
g
is also surjective.

Proof.

We need to shown that for any morphisms
\alpha,\beta
such that
\alpha g=\beta g
,
\alpha
must equal
\beta
. Now note that

\alpha\left(gf\right)=\left(\alpha g\right)f=\left(\beta g\right)f=\beta\left(gf\right).

Since
gf
is surjective, we must have
\alpha=\beta
, proving the claim.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8

We start with the following setup :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota\Omega^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota\Omega^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}

This contains an object
A\in\left[\mathcal{X}\;;\;\Omega\right]
, and two composable arrows
F,F^{\prime}\in\left[\iota\;;\;\iota\right]
. We can apply the functors
\tau_{F}
and
\tau_{F^{\prime}}
in succession to get

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota\Omega^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota\Omega^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{F}(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{F^{\prime}}(b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}

To prove Theorem
8
, it has to be shown that the composition along the arrows in the upper row equals
\tau_{F^{\prime}\circ F}
. The object
\tau_{F^{\prime}\circ F}(a)
itself can be drawn as shown below :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota\Omega^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota\Omega^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!\iota\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{F}(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{F^{\prime}\circ F}(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{F^{\prime}}(b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}

The connecting morphism
\phi:D\to C
exists by the minimality of
\tau_{F^{\prime}\circ F}(a)
. The upper commuting loop can be expressed as

\tau_{F^{\prime}}(b)\circ\tau_{F}(a)=\iota\phi\circ\tau_{F^{\prime}\circ F}(a).

By Theorem
7
, all the three morphisms
\tau_{F}(a)
,
\tau_{F^{\prime}}(b)
and
\tau_{F^{\prime}\circ F}(a)
are surjective. Thus by Lemma
4.2
,
\iota\phi
must be surjective too. By Assumption
3

\iota\phi
is also injective. Thus by Assumption
4
,
\iota\phi
is an isomorphism. This implies that
\tau_{F^{\prime}}(b)\circ\tau_{F}(a)
and
\tau_{F^{\prime}\circ F}(a)
are equal up to isomorphism. This completes the proof of Theorem
8
. ∎

This completes the statement of our main results. The proof of Theorem
1
can now be completed.

Proof of Theorem
1

Note that Assumption
1
in Theorem
1
is a special case of Assumption
5
. As a result we can build the induced functors
\bar{\tau}_{F}
and
\tau_{F}
from Theorems
5
and
6
respectively. Since
\iota
is assumed to be injective on objects, the objects of
\iota(\mathcal{X})
are in bijection with the object of
\mathcal{X}
. Thus each object in
\iota(\mathcal{X})
corresponds to a unique image
\iota\Omega
, for some
\Omega\in ob(\mathcal{X})
. The functoriality now follows from Theorem
8
. This completes the proof of Theorem
1
. ∎

Theorems
5
and
6
remain to be proven. The proofs require building a deeper insight into the inter-relations between comma, arrow, and slice categories. We build this insight over the course of three sections
5
,
6
and
7
. In the next section, we complete the proof of Theorem
1
.

5  Comma and arrow categories

In this section we take a deeper look into the commutation squares in comma categories. We assume throughout this section the general arrangement of (
1
), and the resultant comma category
\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]
. We have seen how an arrow-category is a special instance of a comma category. In this section we are interested in the arrow category of the comma category :
\text{Arrow}\left[\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]\right]
. The objects of this category are commutations of the form

\hbox to88.47pt{\vbox to53.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 44.23201pt\lower-25.49504pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

This first diagram is an object of the comma category

\mathcal{DL}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right):=\left[\Id_{\mathcal{A}}\;;\;\pi_{1}^{\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}\right]

The initials DL indicates ”down-left”, the position of an object of this category relative to an object of
\text{Arrow}\left[\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]\right]
(
9
). Similarly, the upper-right corner is an object of the category

\mathcal{UR}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right):=\left[\pi_{2}^{\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}\;;\;\Id_{\mathcal{B}}\right].

Both the categories
\mathcal{DL}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)
and
\mathcal{UR}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)
can be written more expressively as

(11)

The commutations in (
11
) will be one of the most important theoretical tools in our proofs. The green arrows labeled
U,D,UR,UL,DL,DR
respectively represent the upper, lower, upper-right, upper-left, lower left and lower-right corners of the object in (
9
). The categories
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
also find their place in this diagram as the smallest ingredients of the arrow-comma category
\text{Arrow}\left[\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]\right]
.

We next shift our attention to transformations from one comma category into another.

Comma transformations

Consider a commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}}

(12)

in which functors
I,J,K
connect two comma arrangements
F,G
and
F^{\prime}G^{\prime}
. Then we have

Proposition 9

(Functors between comma categories)
.

Consider the arrangement of categories
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D},\mathcal{E}
and functors
F,G,H,I,J
from (
12
). Then there is an induced functor between comma categories

\Psi_{I,J,K}:\left[F\;;\;G\right]\to\left[F^{\prime}\;;\;G^{\prime}\right],

(13)

where the map between objects and morphisms is as follows :

(15)

The leftmost figure in (
15
) is an object
F
in
\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]
. The middle diagram presents a simple commutation in which this object is re-interpreted as a functor. Finally, the leftmost figure presents an application of Proposition
9
to this commutation. The dashed arrow in the above diagram indicate that it is are defined via composition. Proposition
9
applied to the commutative diagram in the center leads to the functor
\Psi_{\Id_{\mathcal{A}},\Id_{\mathcal{A}},F}
shown on the right. Composition with this functor leads to the functor
\Phi_{F}
shown in green on the right. The top right commutation is a consequence of (
14
). The action of
\Phi_{F}
can be explained simply as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{F}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha x^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha a^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta b^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\phi}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta\psi}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}

The yellow and blue sub-diagrams on the actions of
\Phi_{F}
,
\Phi_{F^{\prime}}
for different objects
F,F^{\prime}\in\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]
.

can be found in the diagram on the left below :

(16)

gain Proposition
9
yields the trivial transformations between three categories, as indicated on the right above. We next use this functorial relation
\Phi_{F}
between categories to study more complicated arrangements.

The dynamics map

The language of comma categories enable complex arrangements of spaces and transformations to be concisely depicted by the comma notation. One a comma category is built, one has two forgetful or projection functors from the comma category. One can then use these functors to build comma categories of a higher level of complexity. We have already seen several examples of this constructive procedure over the course of the diagrams (
10
) and (
11
). Another important instance arises when the functor
\Phi_{F}
from (
15
) is combined with a part of (
11
) to give

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathcal{A}\;;\;a\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Arrow}\left[\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]\right]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\alpha,\beta}}

Now consider any element
a^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}a
from the slice category
\left[\mathcal{A}\;;\;a\right]
, which we just represent as
f
. This can be represented as a functor from the one point category
\star
. This leads to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathcal{A}\;;\;a\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Arrow}\left[\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]\right]}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\alpha,\beta}}

This arrangement is also a diagram in
\left\llbracket\text{Cat}\right\rrbracket
, the category of small categories. As a result we can construct its pull back, which is shown below in green :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\mathcal{Z}\left(f,F\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathcal{A}\;;\;a\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Arrow}\left[\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]\right]}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\alpha,\beta}}

The category
\mathcal{Z}\left(f,F\right)
is the full subcategory of
\text{Arrow}\left[\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]\right]
whose objects are pairs
(F^{\prime},g)
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha a^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta b^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

The upper horizontal arrow can be recovered via the functor
U_{\alpha,\beta}
. This functor can be added to our previous arrangement to get :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\mathcal{Z}\left(f,F\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathcal{A}\;;\;a\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Arrow}\left[\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{f,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\alpha,\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\alpha,\beta}}

(17)

The functor
\zeta_{f,F}
which is created via composition, directly yields the functor we are looking for :

\tau_{F}(f):=\lim\zeta_{f,F}.

(18)

Equation (
18
) is an alternative and easy route to construct the functor
\tau_{F}
from Theorem
5
. However, this construction does not reveal the functorial nature of the correspondence between
f
and
\lim\zeta_{f,F}
. In the following section, we describe a different route to establishing functoriality.

6  Adjointness in commas

In this section the categorical properties of various comma categories and forgetful functors will be examined. The first is a classic result from Category theory :

Lemma 6.1
.

(e.g.
RydeheardBurstall988
, Thm 3)
Let
\alpha:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{C}
and
\beta:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{C}
be functors with
\alpha
(finitely) continuous. If
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are (finitely) complete, then so is the comma category
\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]

One immediate consequence of Lemma
6.1
is :

Lemma 6.2
.

The comma category
\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]
is complete.

We also have :

Lemma 6.3
.

The upper right category
\mathcal{UR}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)
is complete.

Lemma
6.3
follows directly from the construction of
\mathcal{UR}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)
as a comma category in Section
5
, the continuity of the identity functor, and Lemma
6.1
. The following basic lemma is an useful tool in establishing the existence of right adjoints.

Lemma 6.4

(Right inverse as right adjoint)
.

Suppose
F:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
and
G:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{P}
are two functors such that
FG=\Id_{\mathcal{Q}}
and
\Id_{\mathcal{P}}\Rightarrow GF
. Then
F,G
are left and right adjoints of each other.

A slightly harder observation is the following :

Lemma 6.5
.

Consider an arrangement of categories and functors
\mathcal{P}\xrightarrow{P}\mathcal{R}\xleftarrow{Q}\mathcal{Q}
. If
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
have initial elements
0_{\mathcal{P}}
and
1_{\mathcal{Q}}
respectively, and
P\left(0_{\mathcal{P}}\right)=0_{\mathcal{R}}
, then the functor
\pi_{2}:\left[P\;;\;Q\right]
has a left adjoint given by

\left(\pi_{2}\right)^{(L)}:\mathcal{Q}\to\left[P\;;\;Q\right],\quad q\mapsto\;\hbox to59.65pt{\vbox to50.15pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 29.82253pt\lower-25.07495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-29.82253pt}{-19.4708pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

In fact,
\left(\pi_{1}\right)^{(L)}
is a right inverse of
\pi_{1}
, i.e.,
\pi_{2}\circ\left(\pi_{2}\right)^{(L)}=\Id_{\mathcal{Q}}
.

Proof.

Since
\left(\pi_{1}\right)^{(R)}
is a right inverse of
\pi_{1}
by Lemma
6.4
, it only remains to be shown that there is a natural transformation
\left(\pi_{2}\right)^{(L)}\circ\pi_{2}\Rightarrow\Id_{\left[P\;;\;Q\right]}
, called the
counit
. The diagram on the left below traces the action of this composite functor on an object
F
(blue) of
\left[P\;;\;Q\right]
into an object (green) of
\left[P\;;\;Q\right]
:

The connecting morphisms of this natural transformation is shown above on the right.
∎

We end this section with one final observation about the upper-right corner category. Recall the inclusion functor from (
16
). It leads to a pull-back square :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\pi_{2}^{\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}\;;\;b\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{UR}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

Now consider any functor
\mathcal{T}:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{UR}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)
such that
\pi_{2}\circ\mathcal{T}\equiv b
, for some object
b
of
\mathcal{B}
. Then the commutation on the left below is satisfied :

(19)

This commutation must factor through the pullback square as shown by the blue arrow in the diagram in the middle. Thus given any functor
\mathcal{T}
as above, (
19
) says that
\mathcal{T}
factors through a map
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
mapping into
\left[\pi_{2}^{\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}\;;\;b\right]
, a subcategory of
\mathcal{UR}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)
. Since the category
\left[\pi_{2}^{\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}\;;\;b\right]
can be further restricted to the slice
\left[\mathcal{B}\;;\;b\right]
,
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
extends to a functor mapping into
\left[\mathcal{B}\;;\;b\right]
.

7  The Lim-Pre construction

At this stage we can start constructing the functors declared in Theorem
5
and (
8
). These will be constructed by taking various limits. For that purpose we need to established the completeness and continuity of various categories and functors involved. We start with a classic result from Category theory :

Lemma 7.1

(Right adjoints preserve limits)
.

(
Riehl_context_2017
, Thm 4.5.3)
If a functor
F:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
has a left adjoint, then for any diagram
\Psi:J\to\mathcal{P}
, if
\lim\psi
exists, then
\lim(F\circ\Psi)=F\left(\lim\Psi\right)
.

Consider an arrangement
\mathcal{Y}\xleftarrow{F}\mathcal{X}\xrightarrow{G}\mathcal{Z}
. Given an object
y
of
\mathcal{Y}
, one can create the composite functor :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[F\;;\;y\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times\{\star\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\text{Pre}_{F,G}(y)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\left[F\;;\;y\right]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

(20)

This gives us a functor

\text{Pre}_{F,G}:Y\to\left[\left\llbracket\text{Cat}\right\rrbracket\;;\;Z\right],\;y\mapsto\left(\left[F\;;\;y\right],\text{Pre}_{F,G}(y)\right);\;\hbox to26.68pt{\vbox to48.35pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 13.33911pt\lower-24.17558pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{18.5806pt}{-37.4664pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(21)

Now suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is a complete category. The collection of all diagrams in
\mathcal{Z}
, which are functors
F:J\to\mathcal{Z}
is the left slice of
\mathcal{Z}
within
\left\llbracket\text{Cat}\right\rrbracket
the category of small categories. We then have the following result from basic category theory :

Lemma 7.2
.

Given a complete category
\mathcal{Z}
, there is a functor
\lim:\left[\left\llbracket\text{Cat}\right\rrbracket\;;\;\mathcal{Z}\right]\to\mathcal{Z}
which maps each diagram
F:J\to\mathcal{Z}
into
\lim F
.

For a complete category
\mathcal{Z}
, the
\lim
functor can be used to extend the functor from (
21
), as shown below :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\left\llbracket\text{Cat}\right\rrbracket\;;\;Z\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\LimPre_{F,G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lim}

(22)

This construction
\LimPre_{F,G}
is one of the main innovations in this paper. The use of colimits instead of limits would have yielded the right Kan extension of
F
along
G
. for any object
y
of
\mathcal{Y}
,
\LimPre_{F,G}(y)
is the limit point of the functor
G
restricted to the left slice
\left[F\;;\;y\right]
.

Given two complete categories
\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
a functor
\Psi:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
is called
limit preserving
if the following commutation holds :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\left\llbracket\text{Cat}\right\rrbracket\;;\;\mathcal{Z}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\left\llbracket\text{Cat}\right\rrbracket\;;\;\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

(23)

Lemma 7.3
.

Given an arrangement of functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

in which
F
is limit preserving, the following commutation holds between the
\LimPre
functors :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ Q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\LimPre_{R,F\circ Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\LimPre_{R,Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

Proof.

Note that for every
y\in ob(\mathcal{Y})
,

\begin{split}\LimPre_{R,F\circ Q}(y)&=\lim\text{Pre}_{R,F\circ Q}(y)=\lim\text{Pre}_{R,F\circ\text{Pre}_{R,Q}}(y)\\
&=F\circ\lim\text{Pre}_{R,\text{Pre}_{R,Q}}(y)=F\circ\LimPre_{R,Q}(y),\end{split}

where the second last inequality holds from the limit preservation property.
∎

At this point we are ready to prove Theorems
5
and
6
.

8  Proof of Theorems
5
and
6

The proofs of the two theorems shall be derived simultaneously.
We begin the proof by drawing a portion of (
11
).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Arrow}\left[\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{UR}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{DL}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DL}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UR}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}

Since the categories
\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]
and
\mathcal{UR}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)
are complete by Lemmas
6.1
and
6.3
respectively, we can create the
\LimPre
constructions of these functors, as shown below :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Arrow}\left[\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{UR}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{DL}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{UR}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DL}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UR}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\gamma}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}

Each colored dotted arrow is the
\LimPre
construction corresponding to the functor of the same color on the top row. The commutation between the
\LimPre
constructions in the second row holds by Lemma
6.5
, Lemma
6.6
and Lemma
7.3
. We now add some of the peripheral commutations of (
11
) to get

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Arrow}\left[\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{UR}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{DL}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{UR}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DL}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UR}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\gamma}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

Again, by Lemma
6.5
and Lemma
7.3
we can fill in :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Arrow}\left[\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{UR}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{DL}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{UR}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DL}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UR}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\gamma}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

We now add the functor
\Phi_{F}
from (
15
) to this diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Arrow}\left[\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{UR}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{DL}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{UR}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathcal{A}\;;\;a\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DL}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UR}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\gamma}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.99,0.27,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.99,0.27,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.99,0.27,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

Now set
\bar{\tau_{F}}=\bar{\gamma}_{F}\circ\Phi_{F}
and
\tau_{F}=\gamma_{F}\circ\Phi_{F}
. Note that this creates a commutation :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathcal{A}\;;\;a\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{UR}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\tau}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

(24)

This is precisely the commutation described on the left of (
19
). Thus
the conclusions of (
19
) along with (
16
) hold and we get :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Arrow}\left[\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{UR}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{DL}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{UR}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\pi_{2}^{\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}\;;\;b\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathcal{A}\;;\;a\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathcal{B}\;;\;b\right]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DL}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UR}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\gamma}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
Restrict
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\tau}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dyn_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.99,0.27,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.99,0.27,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.99,0.27,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

(25)

The commutations in (
8
) are included within the commutation of (
25
). The claim of minimality in Theorem
5
follows from the construction of
\gamma_{F}
as a limit. The commutation diagram in (
24
) links this minimal comma object to minimal commutation squares completing
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha a^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
.

This completes the proof of Theorems
5
and
6
. ∎
